; Moser estimates for the remaining commutators then give

\big|\langle Q(\nabla\mathcal{U}_{N},\tau_{N}^{0}),\mathcal{T}_{N}\rangle_{H^{s}}\big|\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\nabla\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+C_{T,N}\big(\|\mathcal{U}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\big).

All other transport, commutator and
Q
terms have the same or lower order.
The inhomogeneous damping term satisfies

a|\langle\tau_{N}^{a},\mathcal{T}_{N}\rangle_{H^{s}}|\leq C_{T,N}a^{2}+C\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}.

Consequently,

\frac{d}{dt}\big(\|\mathcal{U}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\big)+\|\nabla\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\leq C_{T,N}\big(\|\mathcal{U}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+a^{2}\big).

The initial difference is zero, so Gronwall’s inequality, Sobolev embedding,
and the corresponding Besov embeddings give

\|(u_{N}^{a}-u_{N}^{0},\tau_{N}^{a}-\tau_{N}^{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\dot{H}^{1}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1})\times L^{\infty}_{T}(\dot{H}^{1}\cap B^{0}_{\infty,1})}\leq C_{T,N}a.

Writing the full difference as the sum of the two tails in
(
7.81
) and this smooth middle difference, and then
letting first
a\to 0
and next
N\to\infty
, proves
(
7.10
). The same difference estimates also complete the
uniqueness and continuous-dependence assertions at the stated regularity.
∎

Remark 7.3

(Persistence of higher Besov regularity)
.

Let
s>0
and retain the divergence, symmetry and velocity-mean assumptions
of Theorem
7.1
. Suppose additionally that

\mathcal{E}_{0,s}:=\|(u_{0},\tau_{0})\|_{H^{1}}+\|(\nabla u_{0},\tau_{0})\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,1}}<\infty.

Higher regularity persists on the lifespan of the critical solution:
for every finite interval
[0,T]
on which that solution exists,

(7.82)

\displaystyle\|u^{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}B^{1+s}_{\infty,1}}+\|\tau^{a}\|_{\widetilde{L}^{\infty}_{T}B^{s}_{\infty,1}}+\|\tau^{a}\|_{L^{1}_{T}B^{2+s}_{\infty,1}}\leq C_{s,T}\mathcal{E}_{0,s}.

The constant depends on the critical bounds on
[0,T]
, but not on
a
.
In particular, if the
basic
norm
\mathcal{E}_{0}
satisfies the
smallness condition of Theorem
7.2
, this holds for every
T<\infty
;
smallness of the additional high norm is not required. Finiteness of

\mathcal{E}_{0,s}
alone does not imply global existence.

Here is the tame estimate underlying this assertion. The positive-index
product law gives

\displaystyle\|u\cdot\nabla\tau\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{s}\big(\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\tau\|_{B^{1+s}_{\infty,1}}+\|\nabla\tau\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u\|_{B^{1+s}_{\infty,1}}\big),

\displaystyle\|Q(\nabla u,\tau)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{s}\big(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\tau\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,1}}+\|\tau\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u\|_{B^{1+s}_{\infty,1}}\big).

In the heat estimate the first term is bounded, for any
\epsilon>0
, by

\epsilon\|\tau\|_{B^{2+s}_{\infty,1}}+C_{s,\epsilon}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\tau\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,1}}
.
The velocity transport and pressure estimates are tame at index
1+s
,
with stress forcing controlled by
\|\tau\|_{B^{2+s}_{\infty,1}}
.
After substituting the heat estimate and absorbing its
\epsilon
term,
Gronwall’s inequality proves (
7.82
). Its
coefficients are integrable on
[0,T]
: in particular,

\|\nabla\tau\|_{L^{1}_{T}L^{\infty}}\leq CT^{1/2}\|\tau\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}B^{0}_{\infty,1}}^{1/2}\|\tau\|_{L^{1}_{T}B^{2}_{\infty,1}}^{1/2}.

This is a finite-time persistence estimate, not a uniform-in-time bound
in higher Besov spaces or an additional same-topology damping-limit claim.

Remark 7.4
.

The restriction to a finite time interval in (
7.9
) is
essential unless additional conditions remove the zero-mode obstruction. For
example, let
u_{0}=0
and let
\tau_{0}\neq 0
be a sufficiently small constant
symmetric matrix. Then

(u^{a}(t),\tau^{a}(t))=(0,e^{-at}\tau_{0}),\qquad(u^{0}(t),\tau^{0}(t))=(0,\tau_{0}).

Thus the difference is
O(aT)
on every fixed interval
[0,T]
, consistently
with Theorem
7.2
, whereas

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|\tau^{a}(t)-\tau^{0}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\|\tau_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}

for every
a>0
. This also explains why the numerical comparisons below are
formulated on bounded time intervals.

Proposition 7.5

(Exponential relaxation on the torus)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.2
, define the spatial average by

\overline{f}:=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{T}^{d}|}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f(x)\,dx.

There exist constants
C,c,\lambda>0
, independent of
a\in[0,1]
, such that

(7.83)

\|u^{a}(t)\|_{H^{1}}+\|\tau^{a}(t)-\overline{\tau^{a}(t)}\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}e^{-\lambda t},\qquad t\geq 0.

The behavior of the stress zero mode is as follows.

(i)

If
a\in(0,1]
, then

(7.84)

\|u^{a}(t)\|_{H^{1}}+\|\tau^{a}(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}e^{-cat},\qquad t\geq 0.

(ii)

If
a=0
, there exists a constant symmetric matrix
\tau_{\infty}
such that

(7.85)

\displaystyle\|u^{0}(t)\|_{H^{1}}+\|\tau^{0}(t)-\tau_{\infty}\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}e^{-\lambda t},\qquad t\geq 0,

\displaystyle\tau_{\infty}=\overline{\tau_{0}}-\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{Q(\nabla u^{0}(s),\tau^{0}(s))}\,ds,\qquad|\tau_{\infty}-\overline{\tau_{0}}|\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}^{2}.

Proof.

The velocity average is conserved. Indeed, periodicity and
\mathop{\rm div}u^{a}=0

give

(7.86)

\frac{d}{dt}\overline{u^{a}}=0.

Set

v=u^{a},\qquad m=\overline{\tau^{a}},\qquad\sigma=\tau^{a}-m.

Thus
v
and
\sigma
have zero spatial mean. Apply the projection

f\mapsto f-\overline{f}
to (
7
). The fluctuation system is

(7.87)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}v+v\cdot\nabla v+\nabla P=\operatorname{div}\sigma,\qquad\operatorname{div}v=0,\\
&\partial_{t}\sigma+v\cdot\nabla\sigma+a\sigma+Q(\nabla v,\sigma)+Q(\nabla v,m)-\overline{Q(\nabla v,\sigma)}=2D(v)+\Delta\sigma.\end{aligned}\right.

The constant term
\overline{Q(\nabla v,\sigma)}
is orthogonal to

\sigma
,
\Delta\sigma
and
\nabla v
. The terms containing
m
obey

\displaystyle|\langle Q(\nabla v,m),\sigma\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C|m|\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}\|\sigma\|_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle|\langle Q(\nabla v,m),\Delta\sigma\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C|m|\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}\|\Delta\sigma\|_{L^{2}},

(7.88)

\displaystyle|\langle Q(\nabla v,m),\nabla v\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C|m|\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Since
|m|\leq C\|\tau\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}
, these terms are
absorbed by the fluctuation dissipation. Repeating
(
7.15
) and (
7.20
) for
(
7.87
) therefore gives an energy

\mathscr{F}_{1}
satisfying

\mathscr{F}_{1}\simeq\|(v,\sigma)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

and

(7.89)

\frac{d}{dt}\mathscr{F}_{1}+c_{0}\big(\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla\sigma\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+a\|\sigma\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\big)\leq 0.

More explicitly,
\mathscr{F}_{1}
is obtained by adding a sufficiently small
multiple of the mixed term
-\langle\sigma,\nabla v\rangle
to the basic

H^{1}
energy. In dimension two,

\langle v\cdot\nabla v,\Delta v\rangle=0
; in dimension three this term is
bounded by

C\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
and is absorbed by the
small global bound from Theorem
7.2
.

By (
7.13
) and the Poincaré inequality for
\sigma
, the
dissipation in (
7.89
) controls
\mathscr{F}_{1}
with a
constant independent of
a
. Consequently,

\mathscr{F}_{1}(t)\leq\mathscr{F}_{1}(0)e^{-2\lambda t},

which proves (
7.83
).

It remains to examine
m
. Averaging the stress equation yields

(7.90)

m^{\prime}(t)+am(t)=-\overline{Q(\nabla v,\sigma)}.

Here we used the bilinearity of
Q
, the fact that
m
is spatially constant,
and
\overline{\nabla v}=0
, which imply

\overline{Q(\nabla v,m)}=0
. By (
7.83
),

(7.91)

|\overline{Q(\nabla v,\sigma)}|\leq C\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}\|\sigma\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}^{2}e^{-2\lambda t}.

For
a>0
, the variation-of-constants formula applied to
(
7.90
) gives

m(t)=e^{-at}\overline{\tau_{0}}-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-a(t-s)}\overline{Q(\nabla v(s),\sigma(s))}\,ds.

Choose
0<c<\min\{1,\lambda\}
. Since
0<a\leq 1
,

\int_{0}^{t}e^{-a(t-s)}e^{-2\lambda s}\,ds\leq Ce^{-cat},

with
C
independent of
a
. Combining this inequality with
(
7.91
) gives
|m(t)|\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}e^{-cat}
uniformly for

a\in(0,1]
. This and (
7.83
) prove
(
7.84
).

When
a=0
, the right-hand side of (
7.90
) is integrable.
Therefore
m(t)
converges to the matrix
\tau_{\infty}
in
(
7.85
), and (
7.91
) also gives

|m(t)-\tau_{\infty}|\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}^{2}e^{-2\lambda t},\qquad|\tau_{\infty}-\overline{\tau_{0}}|\leq C\mathcal{E}_{0}^{2}.

Together with (
7.83
), this completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

Estimate (
7.83
) is a spectral-gap effect. For the
linearization of (
7
), every nonzero Fourier mode of the velocity obeys

\partial_{tt}\widehat{u}(k)+(a+|k|^{2})\partial_{t}\widehat{u}(k)+|k|^{2}\widehat{u}(k)=0,\qquad k\neq 0.

Thus the nonzero modes have a decay rate bounded away from zero uniformly in

a\in[0,1]
. For the homogeneous linearized zero mode (and, in particular,
for a spatially constant stress solution), the stress decays exactly on the
time scale
a^{-1}
when
a>0
and persists when
a=0
. In the nonlinear
system its mean instead satisfies the forced ODE (
7.90
), and
the same slow homogeneous component may remain; the spatially constant
example in Remark
7.4
shows that this rate is
attained. Hence the factor
a
in
(
7.84
) is unavoidable, and the full decay estimate
cannot be uniform as
a\to 0
.

7.2.
Toy models in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
and corresponding semi-implicit numerical schemes

We distinguish the exact co-rotational reduction of (
7
) from the
modified two-component benchmark used in the Gaussian-vortex computations.
Write
u=(u^{1},u^{2})^{\intercal}
and

\omega=\partial_{1}u^{2}-\partial_{2}u^{1}
. For a two-component stress

\tau=(\tau^{1},\tau^{2})^{\intercal}
, define its matrix representation and the
coupling operators by

(7.92)

\mathsf{T}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\tau^{1}&\tau^{2}\\
\tau^{2}&-\tau^{1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{G}\tau=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau^{1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{S}u=\begin{pmatrix}2\partial_{1}u^{1}\\
\partial_{1}u^{2}+\partial_{2}u^{1}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus
\mathsf{G}\tau=\operatorname{div}\mathsf{T}(\tau)
and,
when
\operatorname{div}u=0
,
\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{S}u)=2D(u)
.
For
b=0
, the trace of the matrix stress solves the homogeneous
transport–diffusion–damping equation, since

\operatorname{tr}(\tau\Omega-\Omega\tau)=\operatorname{tr}D(u)=0
.
Consequently trace-free initial stress remains trace free. In this invariant
class, direct computation gives

(7.93)

Q(\nabla u,\mathsf{T}(\tau))=\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{C}(u,\tau)),\qquad\mathsf{C}(u,\tau)=\omega\begin{pmatrix}\tau^{2}\\
-\tau^{1}\end{pmatrix}.

The exact nonlinear co-rotational toy model is therefore

(7.94)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+(u\cdot\nabla)u+\nabla p=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau^{1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathop{\rm div}u=0,\\
\partial_{t}\tau+(u\cdot\nabla)\tau-\Delta\tau+a\tau+\mathsf{C}(u,\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}2\partial_{1}u^{1}\\
\partial_{1}u^{2}+\partial_{2}u^{1}\end{pmatrix},\end{cases}

The nonlinear vanishing-damping table and temporal-convergence plots below
use (
7.94
). The Gaussian nonlinear benchmark instead replaces

\mathsf{C}
by the bilinear closure implemented in the MATLAB driver,

(7.95)

\mathsf{M}(u,\tau)=\omega\begin{pmatrix}-2\tau^{2}\\
\tau^{1}-\tau^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\partial_{t}\tau+(u\cdot\nabla)\tau-\Delta\tau+a\tau+\mathsf{M}(u,\tau)=\mathsf{S}u,

with the same velocity equation as in (
7.94
). This defines a
different nonlinear toy system, not the co-rotational Oldroyd–B reduction:

\mathsf{M}\neq\mathsf{C}
, and
\mathsf{M}
is not a skew rotation of the two
stress components. Theorem
7.2
is not asserted for this modified
closure. Keeping these definitions separate permits an unambiguous reading
of the existing numerical data without changing the computations.
Deleting the transport and bilinear closure terms from either nonlinear
system gives the common linear toy model

(7.96)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+\nabla p=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau^{1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathop{\rm div}u=0,\\
\partial_{t}\tau-\Delta\tau+a\tau=\begin{pmatrix}2\partial_{1}u^{1}\\
\partial_{1}u^{2}+\partial_{2}u^{1}\end{pmatrix}.\end{cases}

We will compute (
7.94
) and (
7.96
) models above by the well-known semi-implicit Fourier spectral method. More precisely speaking, (
7.94
) and (
7.96
) will be computed by the following schemes respectively:

(7.97)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{u_{n+1}-u_{n}}{\Delta t}+\Pi_{K}((u_{n}\cdot\nabla)u_{n+1})+\nabla p_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathop{\rm div}u_{n+1}=0,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n}}{\Delta t}+\Pi_{K}((u_{n}\cdot\nabla)\tau_{n})-\Delta\tau_{n+1}+a\tau_{n+1}+\Pi_{K}\mathsf{C}(u_{n},\tau_{n})=\begin{pmatrix}2\partial_{1}u^{1}_{n}\\
\partial_{1}u^{2}_{n}+\partial_{2}u^{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix},\end{cases}

and

(7.98)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{u_{n+1}-u_{n}}{\Delta t}+\nabla p_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathop{\rm div}u_{n+1}=0,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n}}{\Delta t}-\Delta\tau_{n+1}+a\tau_{n+1}=\begin{pmatrix}2\partial_{1}u^{1}_{n}\\
\partial_{1}u^{2}_{n}+\partial_{2}u^{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix},\end{cases}

subject to the initial data
(\Pi_{K}u_{0},\Pi_{K}\tau_{0})
. Here
(u_{n},\tau_{n})
is the discrete solution at
n
-th time step and
\Delta t
is the time step. For
K\geq 2
, we introduce the space

X_{K}=\text{span}\left\{\cos(k\cdot x)\ ,\ \sin(k\cdot x):\ \ k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ ,\ |k|_{\infty}=\max\{|k_{1}|,|k_{2}|\}\leq K\right\}.

Here
K
denotes the Fourier cutoff, whereas the quoted
N_{x},N_{y}

(and the shorthand
N
in the plots) denote the numbers of grid points.
Then we define
\Pi_{K}
to be the truncation operator of Fourier modes
|k|_{\infty}\leq K
.
\Pi_{K}u_{0},\Pi_{K}\tau_{0}\in X_{K}
and by induction, we have
u_{n},\tau_{n}\in X_{K}\ ,\forall n\geq 0
. Indeed it
is clear that in order to solve
\tau_{n+1}
in (
7.97
) and (
7.98
) the viscosity terms
\Delta\tau_{n+1}
is treated unknown and the nonlinear terms are known from the previous time step. To solve
u_{n+1}
we put
\tau_{n+1}
instead of
\tau_{n}
in the sourcing term; such choice guarantees the regularity balance.

In addition we can further derive that
\mathop{\rm div}u_{n}=0
for all
n
by induction. To solve
u_{n+1}
, we recall the Leray projection
\mathbb{P}
, namely the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto the divergence-free subspace: for any
v\in L^{2}
we have
\mathbb{P}v=v-\nabla q
, where
q\in H^{1}
solves the following Poisson equation under periodic boundary conditions:

\Delta q=\nabla\cdot v.

Then we apply the Leray projection
\mathbb{P}
to derive that

(7.99)

\frac{u_{n+1}-u_{n}}{\Delta t}+\mathbb{P}\Pi_{K}(u_{n}\cdot\nabla u_{n+1})=\mathbb{P}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{1}\end{pmatrix},

or

(7.100)

\frac{u_{n+1}-u_{n}}{\Delta t}=\mathbb{P}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{1}+\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{2}\\
\partial_{1}\tau_{n+1}^{2}-\partial_{2}\tau_{n+1}^{1}\end{pmatrix},

for (
7.97
) and (
7.98
) respectively. The modified
Gaussian benchmark uses the same stress-first scheme with

\mathsf{C}(u_{n},\tau_{n})
replaced by
\mathsf{M}(u_{n},\tau_{n})
in
(
7.97
); the projected velocity step is unchanged.
The Leray projection is evaluated by Fourier multipliers. The damping-rate
computations for the exact co-rotational reduction are consistent with the
finite-time behavior in Theorem
7.2
; the modified Gaussian benchmark
is only an illustrative toy-model experiment. A rigorous
time-discretization error estimate, including uniformity as
a\to 0
,
requires a separate stability analysis and is not asserted here.

Remark 7.7
.

The velocity convection in (
7.97
) and
(
7.99
) is treated semi-implicitly as

(u_{n}\cdot\nabla)u_{n+1}
, cf.
[
11
]
. Both
\mathsf{C}
and

\mathsf{M}
are evaluated explicitly in physical space at
t_{n}
.
The damping-rate and temporal-convergence runs use dealiased products;
the GPU Gaussian benchmark also applies
2/3
Fourier filtering and
solves the semi-implicit velocity equation by GMRES. It inherits the
modified closure
\mathsf{M}
from the MATLAB benchmark, not the
unfiltered MATLAB implementation. These choices are part of the
numerical specification, not a time-discretization stability theorem.

Remark 7.8
.

Indeed we will postpone the discussion of the error analysis in a forthcoming work and only present the dynamics here.

7.3.
Rate of vanishing damping

In this section we present numerical evidence for the rate of vanishing
damping of the schemes (
7.97
) and (
7.98
).
Here the nonlinear calculations use the exact co-rotational model
(
7.94
), not the modified closure (
7.95
).
Theorem
7.2
proves an
L^{2}
estimate of order
a
on fixed time
intervals for sufficiently small matrix data. The
L^{\infty}
slopes reported below
are a stronger numerical observation, and the amplitudes used in these tests
have not been checked against the nonquantitative smallness threshold

\varepsilon_{0}
in that theorem. Throughout this subsection we choose
initial data suggested by
[
11
]
. Since
m=3.8
is not an integer, the ordinary expression
(\cos z)^{m}
is not real-valued when
\cos z<0
. We therefore use the real signed power

c_{m}(z):=\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(\cos z)|\cos z|^{m},\qquad d_{m}(z):=|\cos z|^{m-1},

and set

(7.101)

u_{0}=\tau_{0}=\left(-\frac{m}{2}c_{m}(x)d_{m}(y)\sin y,\frac{m}{2}d_{m}(x)c_{m}(y)\sin x\right),\qquad m=3.8.

In particular,
u_{0}=(\partial_{y}\psi_{m},-\partial_{x}\psi_{m})
with

\psi_{m}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}c_{m}(x)c_{m}(y)
, so the velocity is real-valued, periodic,
and exactly divergence free.

We generate a same-step reference solution

(u_{R},v_{R},\tau^{1}_{R},\tau^{2}_{R})
using the same schemes with

a=0
and
\Delta t=10^{-4}
, and compare it with solutions at different

a
-values. Thus the displayed discrepancies isolate the damping effect at
fixed temporal resolution. A finer-step
a=0
reference would mix damping
with additional time-discretization error; the first-order-in-time check is
recorded separately in Figure
1
.

For reproducibility, let
e=(u^{1}-u_{R},u^{2}-v_{R},\tau^{1}-\tau^{1}_{R},\tau^{2}-\tau^{2}_{R})
on the uniform mesh. The reported errors use

(7.102)

\|e\|_{\infty,h}=\max_{j,\ell}|e^{\ell}(x_{j})|,\qquad\|e\|_{2,h}=\left(\frac{h_{x}h_{y}}{4}\sum_{j}\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}|e^{\ell}(x_{j})|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

The table and plot labels
L^{\infty}
and
L^{2}
refer to these discrete
norms. Thus
\|e\|_{2,h}
is one half of the usual Euclidean joint
four-component
L^{2}
quadrature norm. This component normalization does
not change the observed convergence order; it is not the Frobenius norm
of the lifted matrix stress
\mathsf{T}(\tau)
.

More clearly in the first numerical experiment we compute the linear toy model (
7.96
) using (
7.98
) together with the projected (
7.100
). We fix
\Delta t=0.0001,N_{x}=N_{y}=128
and we vary
a=\frac{1}{2^{k}}
. The
L^{\infty}
-difference at
T=1
is presented in Table
1
. Over the displayed range, the
L^{\infty}
-differences exhibit approximately first-order behavior in
a
.

).

) using (

). We fix

,

, and vary

. They likewise show approximately first-order behavior over the tested values of

).

As a consistency check on the time discretization, we refine
\Delta t
at
fixed
N=128
and
a=0.1
up to
T=0.5
, comparing against a same-grid

first-order decay of the
L^{\infty}
and

errors; see
Figure

.

,

, and

.

7.4.
Dynamics of the linear and modified toy models: Two Gaussian vortices

In this subsection we consider the following initial data of two Gaussian vortices. To make the vorticity genuinely periodic and compatible with

w_{0}=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u_{0}
, we periodize each Gaussian and remove the zero
Fourier mode. Namely, set

G_{(c_{1},c_{2})}(x,y):=\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\exp\left(-5\big((x-c_{1}+2\pi k_{1})^{2}+(y-c_{2}+2\pi k_{2})^{2}\big)\right)

and take the vertical pair used in the MATLAB plates,

(7.103)

w_{0}=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u_{0}=G_{-}+G_{+}-\overline{\bigl(G_{-}+G_{+}\bigr)},\qquad G_{\pm}:=G_{(0,\pm\pi/4)}.

We define
u_{0}
by the zero-mean periodic Biot–Savart inverse of
(
7.103
), with the sign fixed by

\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u_{0}=w_{0}
. Thus
\operatorname{div}u_{0}=0
and

\overline{u_{0}}=0
, as required by the normalization used in the periodic
theory.
Moreover we choose the initial
\tau_{0}
as follows:

\tau_{0}=\left(1-\frac{m}{2}(\cos(x))^{m}(\cos(y))^{m-1}\sin(y),1+\frac{m}{2}(\cos(x))^{m-1}(\cos(y))^{m}\sin(x)\right),

where
m=4
. The constant
1
adds a nonzero background to both reduced
stress components; no small-data threshold has been verified for these
data. This background should not be interpreted as positive definiteness:
the matrix
\mathsf{T}(\tau_{0})
is trace free and is indefinite wherever it
is nonzero. Moreover, the modified closure used below is not covered by
Theorem
7.2
.

We use
N_{x}=N_{y}=512
and
\Delta t=0.001
with the initial data in
(
7.103
). Figures
3
and
3
show the vorticity of the
linear model (
7.96
) and the modified nonlinear benchmark
(
7.95
), respectively. In both plates, rows correspond to

a=0.001,0.1,1
, and columns to
t=0.001,2,10,15
; each panel has its own
color scale. These benchmark examples are motivated by
[
11
]
.
The modified nonlinear run uses (
7.97
) with

\mathsf{C}
replaced by
\mathsf{M}
. The vortices rotate and shear in this
benchmark, whereas the linear snapshots show more diffusive behavior.
Figure
4
records the kinetic enstrophy

E(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\nabla u(t,x)|^{2}\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\omega(t,x)^{2}\,dx,

not the total velocity–stress energy. For
a=0.001
, it rises from about

0.30
to a peak of about
107
near
t=5.1
, then decreases to about
62

at
t=15
; for
a=0.1
the peak is much smaller, and for
a=1
it is already
very small at the tested solver resolution by
t=15
. These finite-resolution observations concern
the modified toy benchmark only and do not establish large-data global
regularity for that benchmark or for the Oldroyd–B system.

Vorticity of the linear model (
7.96
);
N_{x}=N_{y}=512
,
\Delta t=0.001
. Rows:
a=0.001,0.1,1
; columns:
t=0.001,2,10,15
.

), with the same mesh, time step, row and column ordering as Figure
3
.

Kinetic enstrophy
E=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}
of the modified nonlinear benchmark (
7.95
), with
N_{x}=N_{y}=512
and
\Delta t=0.001
.

8.
Conclusion

To conclude, we have established the local well-posedness of the inviscid Oldroyd-B model in the sense of Hadamard with critical regularity. Then we have established the global existence of solutions for
d=2
with damping in the low regularity class
(L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1})\times(L^{2}\cap B^{0}_{\infty,1})
, which is novel in the literature. Furthermore, in both the 2D and 3D cases, we have proved global existence for the inviscid Oldroyd-B model uniformly in the damping parameter. In addition, we have investigated the optimal temporal decay rates and time integrability by improving the existing Fourier splitting method and developing a novel decomposition strategy. One of the major contributions of the present paper is the uniform-in-time vanishing damping limit and the resulting correlation between the sharp vanishing damping rate and the temporal decay rate. On periodic domains, we have further shown exponential relaxation toward spatially homogeneous equilibria. The nonzero Fourier modes decay uniformly in
a\in[0,1]
; for the full stress, the decay rate is of order
a
when
a>0
, whereas an undamped constant stress generally remains when
a=0
. Finally, we have supported our findings with numerical evidence for the vanishing damping limit in the periodic setting.

Several related questions are remaining open after this work.
Our work in the article relies on the smallness of solutions in the critical space. The problem of the global existence of smooth solutions for the 2D inviscid Oldroyd-B model without any smallness assumption is still open. It is also unknown whether there exists a global solution with the critical regularity of the inviscid Oldroyd-B model equation with fractional dissipation
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\tau
, where
\beta\in(0,1)
. We also would like to point out that the global well-posedness problem of the 2D classical Oldroyd-B model for the case
b\neq 0
remains open, as discussed in
[
36
]
. Indeed one possible approach to lower the regularity is to introduce the propagation of randomness, cf.
[
6
,
17
,
18
]
. It is worth pointing out that the critical regularity may become “subcritical” under the probabilistic scaling as suggested by Deng, Nahmod and Yue in
[
18
]
. However, such framework needs delicate adaption in the fluid setting and we shall leave it for the future discussion.
